# South Asia

This article is about the geopolitical subregion of Asia. For the geophysical region of Asia, see [Indian subcontinent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_subcontinent). For the Indian cultural sphere, see [Greater India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_India). For other uses, see [Southern Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Asia_\(disambiguation\)).

**South Asia** is the southern sub-region of [Asia](689.md) that is defined in both geographical and [ethnic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnicity)-[cultural](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture) terms. South Asia, with a population of 2.04 billion, contains a quarter (25%) of the world's population. As commonly conceptualised, the modern [states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_\(polity\)) of South Asia include [Bangladesh](3454.md), [Bhutan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhutan), [India](14533.md), the [Maldives](19117.md), [Nepal](171166.md), [Pakistan](23235.md), and [Sri Lanka](26750.md), with [Afghanistan](737.md) also often included, which may also be classified as part of [Central Asia](6742.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESaez201258-7][^cite_note-8] South Asia borders [East Asia](19605700.md) to the northeast, Central Asia to the northwest, [West Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Asia) to the west and [Southeast Asia](28741.md) to the east. Southeast Asia and [Maritime South Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Littoral_South_Asia) are the only subregions of Asia that lie partly within the [Southern Hemisphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Hemisphere). The [British Indian Ocean Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Indian_Ocean_Territory) and two out of [26 atolls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atolls_of_Maldives) of the Maldives in South Asia lie entirely within the Southern Hemisphere. Topographically, it is dominated by the [Indian subcontinent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_subcontinent) and is bounded by the [Indian Ocean](14580.md) in the south, and the [Himalayas](37966.md), [Karakoram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karakoram), and [Pamir Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pamir_Mountains) in the north.[^cite_note-Chapman,_Graham_P._p._10-9]

[Settled life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic) emerged on the Indian subcontinent in the western margins of the [Indus River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_River) [Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_Basin) 9,000 years ago, evolving gradually into the [Indus Valley Civilisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_Valley_Civilisation) of the third millennium BCE.[^cite_note-Combined-2-10] By 1200 BCE, an [archaic form](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-language) of [Sanskrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanskrit), an [Indo-European language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-European_language), had [diffused](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-cultural_diffusion) into India from the northwest,[^cite_note-Lowe2015-11][^cite_note-Combined-4-Rigveda2-12] with the [Dravidian languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dravidian_languages) being supplanted in the northern and western regions.[^cite_note-Combined-4-13] By 400 BCE, [stratification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_stratification) and [exclusion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_exclusion) by [caste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caste) had emerged within [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism),[^cite_note-Dyson2018-16a-14] and [Buddhism](3267529.md) and [Jainism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jainism) had arisen, proclaiming [social orders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_order) unlinked to heredity.[^cite_note-Fisher2018-59-15]

In the early medieval era, [Christianity](5211.md), [Islam](6037917.md), [Judaism](15624.md), and [Zoroastrianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zoroastrianism) became established on South Asia's southern and western coasts.[^cite_note-Combined-8-16] [Muslim](19541.md) armies from Central Asia intermittently overran the plains of northern India,[^cite_note-Combined-13-17] eventually founding the [Delhi Sultanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi_Sultanate) in the 13th century, and drawing the region into the cosmopolitan [networks of medieval Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Golden_Age).[^cite_note-Combined-10-18] The Islamic [Mughal Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_Empire), in 1526, ushered in two centuries of relative peace,[^cite_note-AsherAsher2006-152-19] leaving a legacy of luminous architecture.[^cite_note-21][^cite_note-Fisher2018-106-20] Gradually expanding [rule of the British East India Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Company_rule_in_India) followed, turning most of South Asia into a colonial economy, but also consolidating its [sovereignty](28791.md).[^cite_note-Combined-11-22] [British Crown rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Raj) began in 1858. The rights promised to Indians were granted slowly,[^cite_note-Combined-12-23][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPeers2013\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksiddyQuAgAAQBAJpgPA76_76\]-24] but [technological changes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_Revolution) were introduced, and modern ideas of education and the public life took root.[^cite_note-EmbreeHay1988-25] In 1947, the [British Indian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Raj) was [partitioned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partition_of_India) into two independent [dominions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominion#India_and_Pakistan),[^cite_note-fisher-partition-26][^cite_note-chatterji-partition-27][^cite_note-talbot-sing-28][^cite_note-khan-great-partition-29] a Hindu-majority [Dominion of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominion_of_India) and a Muslim-majority [Dominion of Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominion_of_Pakistan), amid large-scale loss of life and an unprecedented migration.[^cite_note-30] The 1971 [Bangladesh Liberation War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh_Liberation_War), a [Cold War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_War_in_Asia#South_Asia) episode resulting in [East Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Pakistan)'s secession,[^cite_note-31] was the most recent instance of a new nation being formed in the region.

South Asia has a total area of 5.2 million km<sup>2</sup> (2 million sq.mi), which is 10% of the Asian continent.[^cite_note-EoMA-32] The population of South Asia is estimated to be 2.04 billion[^cite_note-Chapman,_Graham_P._p._10-9] or about one-fourth of the world's population, making it both the [most populous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_continents_and_continental_subregions_by_population) and the [most densely populated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_density) geographical region in the world.[^cite_note-South_Asia_Regional_Overview-33]

In 2022, South Asia had the world's largest populations of [Hindus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindus), [Muslims](19541.md), [Sikhs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikhs), Jains, and Zoroastrians.[^cite_note-dip8Jan2016-34] South Asia alone accounts for 90.47% of Hindus, 95.5% of Sikhs, and 31% of Muslims worldwide, as well as 35 million [Christians](18337522.md) and 25 million Buddhists.[^cite_note-Pew_Research_2012-35][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERuffle2013193-36][^cite_note-pewforum.org-37][^cite_note-pew2Apr2015-38]

The [South Asian Association for Regional Cooperation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Asian_Association_for_Regional_Cooperation) (SAARC) is an economic cooperation organisation in the region which was established in 1985 and includes all of the South Asian nations.[^cite_note-SAARC_Summit-39]

## Definition

See also: [Greater India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_India)

### Ambiguity



Various definitions of South Asia, including the definition by the [United Nations geoscheme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_geoscheme) which was created for "statistical convenience and does not imply any assumption regarding political or other affiliation of countries or territories."

[^cite_note-Millenniumindicators.un.org-40]

The geographical extent is not clear cut as systemic and foreign policy orientations of its constituents are quite asymmetrical.[^cite_note-Ghosh1989-41] Beyond the core territories of the [Indian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Raj) (territories of the former [British Raj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Raj)), there is a high degree of variation as to which other countries are included in South Asia.[^cite_note-42][^cite_note-Razzaque3-43][^cite_note-mmann-44][^cite_note-anderson5-45] There is no clear boundary – geographical, geopolitical, socio-cultural, economical, or historical – between South Asia and other parts of Asia, especially Southeast Asia and West Asia.[^cite_note-46] Most of this region is peninsular, resting on the [Indian Plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Plate) and being isolated from the rest of Asia by mountain barriers;[^cite_note-47][^cite_note-48] it resembles a diamond delineated by the Himalayas on the north, the [Hindu Kush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu_Kush) in the west, and the [Arakan Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chin_Hills) in the east,[^cite_note-Baker2002-49] also extending southward into the Indian Ocean with the [Arabian Sea](2747.md) to the southwest and the [Bay of Bengal](47892.md) to the southeast.[^cite_note-Oxford-50][^cite_note-51]





The northwestern boundary of South Asia is debated, with Afghanistan being variously described as South or Central Asian

The common definition of South Asia is largely inherited from the administrative boundaries of the Indian Empire,[^cite_note-52] with several exceptions. The current territories of [Bangladesh](3454.md), [India](14533.md), and [Pakistan](23235.md) which were the core territories of the [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire) from 1857 to 1947 also form the core territories of South Asia.[^cite_note-The_World_Bank-53][^cite_note-eldis.org-54] [Nepal](171166.md) and [Bhutan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhutan), two independent countries that were British protectorates but not apart of the British Raj,[^cite_note-55] and the [island countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Island_South_Asia) of Sri Lanka and the Maldives are generally included.[^cite_note-McLeod2002-56] By various definitions based on substantially different reasons, the [British Indian Ocean Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Indian_Ocean_Territory) and the [Tibet Autonomous Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tibet_Autonomous_Region) may be included as well.[^cite_note-57][^cite_note-58][^cite_note-59][^cite_note-60][^cite_note-61][^cite_note-62] [Myanmar](19457.md) (Burma), a former British colony and now largely considered a part of Southeast Asia, is also sometimes included.[^cite_note-Ghosh1989-41][^cite_note-Razzaque3-43][^cite_note-mittal-63] [Afghanistan](737.md) is also included by some sources.[^cite_note-Ghosh1989-41][^cite_note-Razzaque3-43][^cite_note-64][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESaez201258-7]

### Organisational definitions



United Nations map of South Asia.

[^cite_note-unmapsworld-65]

&#x20;However, the United Nations does not endorse any definitions or area boundaries.

[\[note 2\]](#cite_note-66)

The [South Asian Association for Regional Cooperation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Asian_Association_for_Regional_Cooperation) (SAARC), a contiguous block of countries, started in 1985 with seven countries – Bangladesh, Bhutan, India, the Maldives, Nepal, Pakistan, and Sri Lanka – and admitted Afghanistan as an eighth member in 2007.[^cite_note-67][^cite_note-SAARC_Summit-39] China and Myanmar have also applied for the status of full members of SAARC.[^cite_note-68][^cite_note-69] The [South Asia Free Trade Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Asia_Free_Trade_Agreement) admitted Afghanistan in 2011.[^cite_note-70]

The [World Bank](36752.md) and [United Nations Children's Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Children's_Fund) (UNICEF) recognise the eight SAARC countries as South Asia,[^cite_note-71][^cite_note-72][^cite_note-73][^cite_note-74] while the [Hirschman–Herfindahl index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herfindahl_index) of the [United Nations Economic and Social Commission for Asia and the Pacific](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Economic_and_Social_Commission_for_Asia_and_the_Pacific) for the region excludes Afghanistan from South Asia.[^cite_note-75] The Population Information Network (POPIN) excludes Maldives which is included as a member of Pacific POPIN subregional network.[^cite_note-76] The United Nations Statistics Division's [scheme of subregions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_geoscheme), for statistical purpose,[^cite_note-Millenniumindicators.un.org-40] includes [Iran](14653.md) along with all eight members of the SAARC as part of [Southern Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Asia_\(UN_geoscheme\)).[^cite_note-77]

### Indian subcontinent

See also: [Indian subcontinent § Geopolitics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_subcontinent#Geopolitics)



The region as described in a 1992 work about the geography of Asia: *"This greater India is well defined in terms of topography; it is the Indian peninsula, hemmed in by the Himalayas on the north, the Hindu Khush in the west and the Arakanese in the east."*

[^cite_note-mittal-63]

[^cite_note-Baker2002-49]

The terms "[Indian subcontinent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_subcontinent)" and "South Asia" are sometimes used interchangeably.[^cite_note-McLeod2002-56][^cite_note-Oxford-50][^cite_note-norwineplus-78][^cite_note-BoseJalal2004-79][^cite_note-80] The Indian subcontinent is largely a geological term referring to the land mass that drifted northeastwards from ancient [Gondwana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gondwana), colliding with the Eurasian plate nearly 55 million years ago, towards the end of [the Palaeocene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paleocene). This geological region largely includes Bangladesh, Bhutan, India, Maldives, Nepal, Pakistan, and Sri Lanka.[^cite_note-Jones2011p267-81] Historians [Catherine Asher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catherine_Asher) and Cynthia Talbot state that the term "Indian subcontinent" describes a natural physical landmass in South Asia that has been relatively isolated from the rest of Eurasia.[^cite_note-AsherTalbot2006-82]

The use of the term *Indian subcontinent* began in the British Empire, and has been a term particularly common in its successors.[^cite_note-norwineplus-78] South Asia as the preferred term is particularly common when scholars or officials seek to differentiate this region from East Asia.[^cite_note-inden-83] According to historians [Sugata Bose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sugata_Bose) and [Ayesha Jalal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayesha_Jalal), the Indian subcontinent has come to be known as South Asia "in more recent and neutral parlance."[^cite_note-BoseJalal2004-79] This "neutral" notion refers to the concerns of Pakistan and Bangladesh, particularly given the recurring conflicts between India and Pakistan, wherein the dominant placement of "India" as a prefix before the subcontinent might offend some political sentiments.[^cite_note-mittal-63] In Pakistan, even the term "South Asia" was considered too India-centric and was "banned" within the International Relations department of Karachi University until 1989 after the death of [Zia ul Haq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zia_ul_Haq).[^cite_note-84] This region has also been labelled as "India" (in its classical and [pre-modern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_kingdoms_of_India) sense) and "[Greater India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_India)".[^cite_note-mittal-63][^cite_note-Baker2002-49]

### Asian context

See also: [Akhand Bharat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akhand_Bharat) and [Foreign relations of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_India)







The [Greater Middle East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Middle_East) (top), [Central Asia](6742.md) and associated neighbouring regions (middle), and [East Asia](19605700.md) (bottom)

[^cite_note-85]

According to Robert M. Cutler – a scholar of political science at [Carleton University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carleton_University), the terms South Asia, Southwest Asia, and Central Asia are distinct, but the confusion and disagreements have arisen due to the geopolitical movement to enlarge these regions into Greater South Asia, [Greater Southwest Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Middle_East), and [Greater Central Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Central_Asia). The frontier of Greater South Asia, states Cutler, between 2001 and 2006 has been geopolitically extended to eastern Iran and western Afghanistan in the west, and in the north to northeastern Iran, northern Afghanistan, and southern [Uzbekistan](31853.md).[^cite_note-Amineh2007p112-86]

Identification with a South Asian identity was found to be significantly low among respondents in an older two-year survey across Bangladesh, India, Nepal, Pakistan, and Sri Lanka.[^cite_note-87]

## History

For a topical guide, see [Outline of South Asian history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outline_of_South_Asian_history). For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of South Asian history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_South_Asian_history).

For the pre-1947 history of the region, see [History of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_India), [History of Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Pakistan), and [History of Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Bangladesh).

### Pre-history

See also: [Peopling of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peopling_of_India)

The history of core South Asia begins with evidence of human activity of *[Homo sapiens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatomically_modern_humans),* as long as 75,000 years ago, or with earlier hominids including *[Homo erectus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homo_erectus)* from about 500,000 years ago.[^cite_note-88] The earliest prehistoric culture have roots in the [Mesolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesolithic) sites as evidenced by the rock paintings of [Bhimbetka rock shelters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhimbetka_rock_shelters) dating to a period of 30,000 BCE or older,[^cite_note-89] as well as Neolithic times.[^cite_note-90]

### Ancient era



[Indus Valley Civilisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_Valley_Civilisation) during 2600–1900 BCE, the mature phase

The [Indus Valley Civilisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_Valley_Civilisation), which spread and flourished in the northwestern part of South Asia from c. 3300 to 1300 BCE in present-day [Pakistan](23235.md), [North India](1552939.md), and [Afghanistan](737.md), was the first major civilisation in South Asia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThapar196623-91] A sophisticated and technologically advanced urban culture developed in the [Mature Harappan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mature_Harappan) period, from 2600 to 1900 BCE.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThapar196624-92] According to anthropologist [Possehl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gregory_Possehl), the Indus Valley Civilisation provides a logical, if somewhat arbitrary, starting point for South Asian religions, but these links from the Indus religion to later-day South Asian traditions are subject to scholarly dispute.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPossehl2002141–156-93]



Maurya Empire in 265 BCE

The Vedic period, named after the Vedic religion of the [Indo-Aryans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Aryan_peoples),[^cite_note-95] lasted from c. 1900 to 500 BCE.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMichaels200432-96][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWitzel19953-4-97] The Indo-Aryans were [Indo-European](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-Indo-Europeans)-speaking pastoralists[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWitzel1995-98] who migrated into north-western India after the collapse of the Indus Valley Civilisation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMichaels200433-94][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFlood199630–35-99] Linguistic and archaeological data show a cultural change after 1500 BCE,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMichaels200433-94] with the linguistic and religious data showing links with Indo-European languages and religion.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFlood199633-100] By about 1200 BCE, the Vedic culture and agrarian lifestyle were established in the northwest and northern Gangetic plain of South Asia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWitzel1995-98][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESamuel200843–45,_48-101] Rudimentary state-forms appeared, of which the [Kuru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuru_Kingdom)-Pañcāla union was the most influential.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWitzel19956-102][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESamuel200852–53-103] The first recorded [state-level society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_\(polity\)) in South Asia existed around 1000 BCE.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWitzel1995-98] The [Brahmanas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brahmana) and [Aranyakas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aranyaka), and the [Upanishads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upanishads) into which they merged, began to ask the meaning of a ritual, adding increasing levels of philosophical and metaphysical speculation,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESamuel200825-104] or "[Hindu synthesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu_synthesis)".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHiltebeitel200712-105]

Increasing urbanisation of South Asia between 800 and 400 BCE, and possibly the spread of urban diseases, contributed to the rise of ascetic movements and of new ideas which challenged the orthodox [Brahmanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brahmanism).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFlood199681–82-106] These ideas led to [Śramana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sramana) movements, of which [Mahavira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahavira) (c. 549–477 BCE), proponent of [Jainism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jainism), and [Buddha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddha) (c. 563 – c. 483), founder of [Buddhism](3267529.md), were the most prominent icons.[^cite_note-World_Religions-107]

The Greek army led by [Alexander the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_the_Great) stayed in the [Hindu Kush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu_Kush) region of South Asia for several years and then later moved into the Indus valley region. Later, the [Maurya Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maurya_Empire) extended over much of South Asia in the 3rd century BCE. Buddhism spread beyond South Asia, through northwest into Central Asia. The [Bamiyan Buddhas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bamiyan_Buddhas) of Afghanistan and the [Edicts of Ashoka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edicts_of_Ashoka) suggest that the Buddhist monks spread Buddhism (Dharma) in eastern provinces of the [Seleucid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seleucid_Empire), and possibly even farther into West Asia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGombrich2006135-108][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETrainor2004103,_119-109][^cite_note-FOOTNOTENeelis2011102–106-110] The Theravada school spread south from India in the 3rd century BCE, to Sri Lanka, later to Southeast Asia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGuy20149–11,_14–15,_19–20-111] Buddhism, by the last centuries of the 1st millennium BCE, was prominent in the Himalayan region, Gandhara, Hindu Kush region, and Bactria.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENeelis2011114–115,_144,_160–163,_170–176,_249–250-112][^cite_note-deborahkh-113][^cite_note-114]

From about 500 BCE through about 300 CE, the Vedic-Brahmanic synthesis or "Hindu synthesis" continued.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHiltebeitel200712-105] Classical Hindu and Sramanic (particularly Buddhist) ideas spread within South Asia, as well as outside South Asia.[^cite_note-115][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESamuel200876–78,_194–201-116][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGuy201410–11-117] The [Gupta Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gupta_Empire) ruled over a large part of the region between the 4th and 7th centuries, a period that saw the construction of major temples, monasteries and universities such as the [Nalanda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nalanda).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMichell197718,_40-118][^cite_note-scharfe2002p144-119][^cite_note-120] During this era, and through the 10th century, numerous cave monasteries and temples such as the [Ajanta Caves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ajanta_Caves), [Badami cave temples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Badami_cave_temples), and [Ellora Caves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ellora_Caves) were built in South Asia.[^cite_note-121][^cite_note-122][^cite_note-123]

### Medieval era

See also: [Medieval India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval_India)



Outreach of influence of early medieval [Chola dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chola_dynasty)

Islam came as a political power in the fringe of South Asia in 8th century CE when the Arab general [Muhammad bin Qasim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_bin_Qasim) conquered [Sindh](28972.md) and [Multan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multan) in southern Punjab, in modern-day Pakistan.[^cite_note-infopak-124] By 962 CE, Hindu and Buddhist kingdoms in South Asia were under a wave of raids from Muslim armies from Central Asia.[^cite_note-mrpislam-125] Among them was [Mahmud of Ghazni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmud_of_Ghazni), who raided and plundered kingdoms in north India from east of the Indus river to west of Yamuna river seventeen times between 997 and 1030.[^cite_note-pj03-126] Mahmud of Ghazni raided the treasuries but retracted each time, only extending Islamic rule into western Punjab.[^cite_note-127][^cite_note-128]



[Timur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timur) defeats the [Sultan of Delhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultan_of_Delhi), Nasir-u Din Mehmud, in the winter of 1397–1398

The wave of raids on north Indian and western Indian kingdoms by Muslim warlords continued after Mahmud of Ghazni, plundering and looting these kingdoms.[^cite_note-129] The raids did not establish or extend permanent boundaries of their Islamic kingdoms. The Ghurid Sultan [Mu'izz al-Din Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mu'izz_al-Din_Muhammad) began a systematic war of expansion into North India in 1173.[^cite_note-130] He sought to carve out a principality for himself by expanding the Islamic world,[^cite_note-pj03-126][^cite_note-131] and thus laid the foundation for the Muslim kingdom that became the [Delhi Sultanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi_Sultanate).[^cite_note-pj03-126] Some historians chronicle the Delhi Sultanate from 1192 due to the presence and geographical claims of Mu'izz al-Din in South Asia by that time.[^cite_note-132]

The Delhi Sultanate covered varying parts of South Asia and was ruled by a series of dynasties: [Mamluk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamluk_dynasty_\(Delhi\)), [Khalji](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khalji_dynasty), [Tughlaq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tughlaq_dynasty), [Sayyid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sayyid_dynasty), and [Lodi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lodi_dynasty) dynasties. [Muhammad bin Tughlaq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_bin_Tughlaq) came to power in 1325, launched a war of expansion and the Delhi Sultanate reached it largest geographical reach over the South Asian region during his 26-year rule.[^cite_note-ebmit-133] A Sunni Sultan, Muhammad bin Tughlaq persecuted non-Muslims such as Hindus, as well as non-Sunni Muslims such as Shia and Mahdi sects.[^cite_note-134][^cite_note-vsoxfordmbt-135][^cite_note-136]

Revolts against the Delhi Sultanate sprang up in many parts of South Asia during the 14th century. In the northeast, the [Bengal Sultanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengal_Sultanate) became independent in 1346 CE. It remained in power through the early 16th century. The state religion of the sultanate was Islam.[^cite_note-Lewis2011-137][^cite_note-Hussain2003-138] In [South India](678583.md), the Hindu [Vijayanagara Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vijayanagara_Empire) came to power in 1336 and persisted until the middle of the 16th century. It was ultimately defeated and destroyed by an alliance of the Muslim [Deccan sultanates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deccan_sultanates) at the [Battle of Talikota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Talikota).[^cite_note-139][^cite_note-140]

About 1526, the Punjab governor [Daulat Khan Lodi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daulat_Khan_Lodi) reached out to the [Mughal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_people) [Babur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babur) and invited him to attack the Delhi Sultanate. Babur defeated and killed [Ibrahim Lodi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibrahim_Khan_Lodi) in the [Battle of Panipat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Panipat_\(1526\)) in 1526. The death of Ibrahim Lodi ended the Delhi Sultanate, and the [Mughal Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_Empire) replaced it.[^cite_note-eblodi-141]

### Modern era



Emperor [Shah Jahan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shah_Jahan) and his son Prince [Aurangzeb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aurangzeb) in Mughal court, 1650

The [modern history period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_era) of South Asia, that is the 16th century onwards, witnessed the establishment of the Mughal Empire, with Sunni Islam theology. The first ruler was Babur had [Turco-Mongol roots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turco-Mongol_tradition) and his realm included the northwestern and [Indo-Gangetic Plain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Gangetic_Plain) regions of South Asia. Several regions of South Asia were largely under [Hindu kings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu_king) such as those of the [Vijayanagara Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vijayanagara_Empire) and the [Kingdom of Mewar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Mewar).[^cite_note-142] Parts of modern [Telangana](990267.md) and [Andhra Pradesh](2377.md) were under local Muslim rulers, the Deccan Sutanates.

The Mughal Empire continued its wars of expansion after Babur's death. With the fall of the Rajput kingdoms and Vijayanagara, its boundaries encompassed almost the entirety of the Indian subcontinent.[^cite_note-borocz-143] The Mughal Empire was marked by a period of artistic exchanges and a Central Asian and South Asian architecture synthesis, with remarkable buildings such as the [Taj Mahal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taj_Mahal).[^cite_note-144][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaddison2003259–261-145][^cite_note-harrison-146]

However, this time also marked an extended period of [religious persecution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_violence_in_India#Mughal_Empire).[^cite_note-147] Two of the religious leaders of [Sikhism](27964.md), [Guru Arjan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Arjan), and [Guru Tegh Bahadur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Tegh_Bahadur) were arrested under orders of the Mughal emperors after their revolts and were executed when they refused to convert to Islam.[^cite_note-148][^cite_note-149][^cite_note-150] Religious taxes on non-Muslims called *[jizya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jizya)* were imposed. Buddhist, Hindu, and Sikh temples were desecrated. However, not all Muslim rulers persecuted non-Muslims. [Akbar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akbar), a Mughal ruler for example, sought religious tolerance and abolished jizya.[^cite_note-151][^cite_note-152][^cite_note-153][^cite_note-154]

The death of [Aurangzeb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aurangzeb) and the collapse of the Mughal Empire, which marks the beginning of modern India, in the early 18th century, provided opportunities for the [Marathas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maratha_Empire), [Sikhs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikh_Empire), [Mysoreans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mysore_Kingdom), and [Nawabs of Bengal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nawabs_of_Bengal) to exercise control over large regions of the Indian subcontinent.[^cite_note-155][^cite_note-156] By the mid-18th century, India was a major [proto-industrializing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-industrialization) region.[^cite_note-voss-157][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaddison2003259–261-145]



British Indian Empire in 1909. [British India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_India) is shaded pink, the [princely states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princely_state) are shaded yellow.

Maritime trading between South Asia and European merchants began at the turn of the 16th century after the [Portuguese discovery of the sea route to India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_discovery_of_the_sea_route_to_India). British, French, and Portuguese colonial interests struck treaties with these rulers and established their trading ports. In northwestern South Asia, a large region was consolidated into the Sikh Empire by [Ranjit Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ranjit_Singh).[^cite_note-158][^cite_note-159] After the defeat of the [Nawab of Bengal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nawab_of_Bengal) and [Tipu Sultan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tipu_Sultan) and his [French allies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franco-Indian_Alliances), [British traders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Company_rule_in_India) went on to dominate much of South Asia through [divide and rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divide_and_rule) tactics by the early 19th century. The region experienced significant [de-industrialisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/De-industrialisation_of_India) in its first few decades of British rule.[^cite_note-\:1-160] Control over the subcontinent was then [transferred](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Raj) to the British government after the [Indian Rebellion of 1857](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Rebellion_of_1857), with the British cracking down to some extent afterwards.[^cite_note-161]

An increase of famines and extreme poverty characterised the colonial period, though railways built with British technology eventually provided crucial famine relief by increasing food distribution throughout India.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMetcalfMetcalf200697-162][^cite_note-\:1-160] Millions of South Asians began to migrate [throughout the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Asian_diaspora#Colonial_era), impelled by the economic/[labour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_indenture_system) needs and opportunities presented by the British Empire.[^cite_note-163][^cite_note-164][^cite_note-165] The introduction of [Western political thought](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_values_\(West\)) inspired a growing Indian intellectual movement, and so by the 20th century, British rule began to be challenged by the [Indian National Congress](149333.md), which sought full independence under the leadership of [Mahatma Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahatma_Gandhi).[^cite_note-D_Demy_2017_p._644-166][^cite_note-167]

Britain, under pressure from Indian freedom fighters, increasingly gave self-rule to British India. By the 1940s, two rival camps had emerged among independence activists: those who [favored a separate nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_nationalism_in_South_Asia) for Indian Muslims, and those who [wanted a united India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Composite_nationalism). As [World War II](32927.md) raged, over 2 million Indians fought for Britain;[^cite_note-Darwin2011-168] by the end of the war, Britain was greatly weakened, and thus decided to grant independence to the vast majority of South Asians in 1947,[^cite_note-Darwin2011-168][^cite_note-169] though this coincided with the [Partition of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partition_of_India) into a Hindu-majority India and a Muslim-majority Pakistan, which resulted in significant displacement and violence and harder religious divides in the region.[^cite_note-170]

### Contemporary era



Indian (brown) and Pakistani (green) territory on 15 August 1947, upon independence.



India and Pakistan consolidated their territory in the mid-20th century, but have an ongoing dispute over [Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashmir).

&#x20;In 1947, the newly independent India and Pakistan had to decide how to deal with the hundreds of [princely states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princely_states) that controlled much of the subcontinent, as well as what to do with the remaining European (non-British) colonies.[^cite_note-171] A combination of referendums, military action, and negotiated accessions took place in rapid succession, leading to the political integration of the [vast majority of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_integration_of_India) and [Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princely_states_of_Pakistan) within a few years.[^cite_note-172][^cite_note-173]



South Asia on the eve of the de jure commencement of the [1971 Indo-Pakistani war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Pakistani_war_of_1971), which concluded with [East Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Pakistan) becoming the independent nation of [Bangladesh](3454.md).

India and Pakistan [clashed several times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Pakistani_wars_and_conflicts) in the decades after independence, with [disputes over Kashmir](3030955.md) playing a significant role.[^cite_note-174] In 1971, the eastern half of Pakistan seceded with help from India and became the People's Republic of Bangladesh after the traumatic [Bangladesh Liberation War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh_Liberation_War).[^cite_note-175] This, along with India and Pakistan gaining nuclear weapons soon afterwards, increased tensions between the two countries.[^cite_note-176] The [Cold War](325329.md) decades also [contributed to the divide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_War_in_Asia#South_Asia), as Pakistan aligned with the West and India with the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union);[^cite_note-177][^cite_note-178] other factors include the time period after the 1962 [Sino-Indian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sino-Indian_War), which saw India and China move apart while Pakistan and China built closer relations.[^cite_note-179]

Pakistan has been beset with [terrorism](2816470.md), [economic issues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poverty_in_Pakistan), and [military dominance of its government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_coups_in_Pakistan) since independence,[^cite_note-180] with none of its Prime Ministers having completed a full 5-year term in office.[^cite_note-181] India has grown significantly,[^cite_note-182] having slashed its rate of extreme poverty to below 20%[^cite_note-\:2-183] and surpassed Pakistan's GDP per capita in the 2010s due to [economic liberalisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_liberalisation_in_India) from the 1980s onward.[^cite_note-\:4-184] Bangladesh, having struggled greatly for decades due to conflict with and economic exploitation by Pakistan,[^cite_note-185][^cite_note-186] is now one of the fastest-growing countries in the region, beating India in terms of [GDP per capita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GDP_per_capita).[^cite_note-187][^cite_note-188] Afghanistan has gone through [several invasions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invasions_of_Afghanistan#Soviet_invasions:_1929,_1930,_and_1979) and Islamist regimes, with many of its refugees having gone to Pakistan and other parts of South Asia and bringing back cultural influences such as [cricket](25675557.md).[^cite_note-189][^cite_note-\:3-190] [Religious nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_nationalism) has grown across the region,[^cite_note-191][^cite_note-dip21Aug2023-192] with persecution causing millions of Hindus and Christians to flee Pakistan and Bangladesh,[^cite_note-193][^cite_note-194] and [Hindu nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindutva) having grown in India with the election of the [Bharatiya Janata Party](149330.md) in 2014.[^cite_note-dip21Aug2023-192]

A recent phenomenon has been that of India and China [fighting on their border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sino-Indian_border_dispute), as well as vying for [dominance of South Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/String_of_Pearls_\(Indian_Ocean\)), with China partnering with Pakistan and using its superior economy to attract countries surrounding India, while [the United States and other countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quadrilateral_Security_Dialogue) have strengthened ties with India to counter China in the broader [Indo-Pacific](330456.md).[^cite_note-195][^cite_note-196] (See also: [2025 India–Pakistan standoff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_India%E2%80%93Pakistan_standoff))

## Geography

See also: [Indian subcontinent § Geography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_subcontinent#Geography)

Further information: [Geography of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_India), [Geography of Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Pakistan), [Geography of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Afghanistan), [Geography of Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Bangladesh), [Geography of Bhutan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Bhutan), [Geography of Sri Lanka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Sri_Lanka), [Geography of Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Nepal), and [Geography of the Maldives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_the_Maldives)

According to Saul Cohen, early colonial era strategists treated South Asia with East Asia, but in reality, the South Asia region excluding Afghanistan is a distinct [geopolitical](163225.md) region separated from other nearby geostrategic realms, one that is geographically diverse.[^cite_note-197] The region is home to a variety of geographical features, such as [glaciers](12463.md), [rainforests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rainforest), [valleys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valley), [deserts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desert), and [grasslands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grassland) that are typical of much larger continents. It is surrounded by three water bodies – the [Bay of Bengal](47892.md), the Indian Ocean and the [Arabian Sea](2747.md) – and has acutely varied climate zones. The tip of the Indian Peninsula had the highest quality pearls.[^cite_note-198]

### Indian Plate

Main article: [Indian Plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Plate)

Most of this region is resting on the [Indian Plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Plate), the northerly portion of the [Indo-Australian Plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Australian_Plate), separated from the rest of the [Eurasian Plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasian_Plate). The Indian Plate includes most of South Asia, forming a land mass which extends from the [Himalayas](37966.md) into a portion of the basin under the Indian Ocean, including parts of [South China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southwest_China) and Eastern [Indonesia](14579.md), as well as [Kunlun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kunlun_Mountains) and [Karakoram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karakoram) ranges,[^cite_note-199][^cite_note-200] and extending up to but not including [Ladakh](303611.md), [Kohistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kohistan_District,_Khyber-Pakhtunkhwa), the [Hindu Kush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu_Kush) range, and [Balochistan](8431743.md).[^cite_note-201][^cite_note-202][^cite_note-203] It may be noted that [geophysically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geophysics) the [Yarlung Tsangpo River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yarlung_Tsangpo_River) in Tibet is situated at the outside the border of the regional structure, while the [Pamir Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pamir_Mountains) in Tajikistan are situated inside that border.[^cite_note-204]

The [Indian subcontinent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_subcontinent) formerly formed part of the [supercontinent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supercontinent) [Gondwana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gondwana), before rifting away during the [Cretaceous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cretaceous) period and colliding with the Eurasian Plate about 50–55 million years ago and giving birth to the [Himalayan range](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Himalayan_range) and the [Tibetan Plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tibetan_Plateau). It is the [peninsular](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peninsula) region south of the [Himalayas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Himalaya) and [Kuen Lun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuen_Lun) mountain ranges and east of the [Indus River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_River) and the [Iranian Plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_Plateau), extending southward into the Indian Ocean between the Arabian Sea (to the southwest) and the Bay of Bengal (to the southeast).

### Climate



South Asia's [Köppen climate classification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification) map

[^cite_note-Peel-205]

&#x20;is based on native vegetation, temperature, precipitation, and their seasonality.&#x20;

&#x20; [(Af) **Tropical rainforest**](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_rainforest_climate)

&#x20; [(Am) **Tropical monsoon**](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_monsoon_climate)

&#x20; [(Aw) **Tropical savanna**, wet & dry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_savanna_climate)

&#x20; [(BWh) **Hot desert**](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hot_desert_climate)

&#x20; [(BWk) **Cold desert**](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_desert_climate)

&#x20; [(BSh) **Hot semi arid**](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hot_semi-arid_climate)

&#x20; [(BSk) **Cold semi arid**](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_semi-arid_climate)

&#x20; [(Csa) **Mediterranean**, dry, hot summer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hot-summer_Mediterranean_climate)

&#x20; [(Cfa) **Subtropical**, humid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humid_subtropical_climate)

&#x20; [(Cwa) **Subtropical**, humid summer, dry winter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humid_subtropical_climate)

&#x20; [(Cwb) **Subtropical highland**, dry winter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subtropical_highland_climate)

&#x20; [(Dsa) **Continental**, hot summer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humid_continental_climate)

&#x20; [(Dsb) **Continental**, warm summer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humid_continental_climate)

&#x20; [(Dwb) **Continental**, dry winter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humid_continental_climate)

&#x20; [(Dwc) **Continental Subarctic**, dry winter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subarctic_climate)

The climate of this vast region varies considerably from area to area from tropical monsoon in the south to temperate in the north. The variety is influenced by not only the altitude but also by factors such as proximity to the seacoast and the seasonal impact of the [monsoons](57630.md). Southern parts are mostly hot in summers and receive rain during monsoon periods. The northern belt of [Indo-Gangetic plains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Gangetic_plain) also is hot in summer, but cooler in winter. The mountainous north is colder and receives snowfall at higher altitudes of Himalayan ranges.

As the [Himalayas](37966.md) block the north-Asian bitter cold winds, the temperatures are considerably moderate in the plains down below. For the most part, the climate of the region is called the monsoon climate, which keeps the region humid during summer and dry during winter, and favours the cultivation of [jute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jute), tea, rice, and various vegetables in this region.

South Asia is largely divided into four broad climate zones:[^cite_note-olive1-206]

- The northern Indian edge and northern Pakistani uplands have a dry subtropical [continental climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_climate)

- The far south of India and southwest Sri Lanka have an [equatorial climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equatorial_climate)

- Most of the peninsula has a tropical climate with variations:&#x20;

  - Hot [subtropical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subtropics) climate in northwest India

  - Cool winter hot [tropical climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_climate) in Bangladesh

  - Tropical [semi-arid climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semi-arid_climate) in the center

- The Himalayas and most of the [Hindu Kush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu_Kush) have an [Alpine climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alpine_climate)

Maximum relative humidity of over 80% has been recorded in [Khasi and Jaintia Hills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khasi_and_Jaintia_Hills) and Sri Lanka, while the area adjustment to Pakistan and western India records lower than 20%–30%.[^cite_note-olive1-206] Climate of South Asia is largely characterised by monsoons. South Asia depends critically on monsoon rainfall.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMitraKumarKumarAbrol200283-207] Two monsoon systems exist in the region:[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMitraKumarKumarAbrol200276-208]

- The summer monsoon: Wind blows from the southwest to most parts of the region. It accounts for 70%–90% of the annual precipitation.

- The winter monsoon: Wind blows from the northeast. Dominant in Sri Lanka and Maldives.

The warmest period of the year precedes the monsoon season (March to mid June). In the summer the low pressures are centered over the [Indus-Gangetic Plain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus-Gangetic_Plain) and high wind from the Indian Ocean blows towards the center. The monsoons are the second coolest season of the year because of high humidity and cloud covering. But, at the beginning of June, the jetstreams vanish above the [Tibetan Plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tibetan_Plateau), low pressure over the [Indus Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_Valley) deepens and the [Intertropical Convergence Zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intertropical_Convergence_Zone) (ITCZ) moves in. The change is violent. Moderately vigorous monsoon depressions form in the Bay of Bengal and make landfall from June to September.[^cite_note-olive1-206]

#### Climate change

Main article: [Climate change in Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_in_Asia)



Greater warming increases the amount of moisture in the atmosphere over Asia, which directly leads to extreme precipitation. Probability of 20-year, 50-year and 100-year extremes consistently increases with warming across Asia. In the most affected parts of South Asia, up to a 15-fold average increase for 100-year extremes is possible under 3 °C (5.4 °F) of warming.

[^cite_note-Guo2023-209]

South Asian countries are expected to experience more flooding in the future as the monsoon pattern intensifies.[^cite_note-AR6_WGII_Chapter10-210]<sup>: 1459 </sup> Across Asia as a whole, 100-year extremes in vapour transport (directly related to extreme precipitation) would become 2.6 times more frequent under 1.5 °C (2.7 °F) of global warming, yet 3.9 and 7.5 times more frequent under 2 °C (3.6 °F) and 3 °C (5.4 °F). In parts of South Asia, they could become up to 15 times more frequent.[^cite_note-Guo2023-209] At the same time, up to two-thirds of glacier ice in the Hindu Kush region may melt by 2100 under high warming, and these glaciers feed the water basin of over 220 million people.[^cite_note-AR6_WGII_Chapter10-210]<sup>: 1487 </sup> As glacier meltwater flow diminishes after 2050, [Hydropower](14073.md) generation would become less predictable and reliable, while agriculture would become more reliant on the intensified monsoon than ever before.[^cite_note-\:HKH2019Climate-211][^cite_note-\:HKH2019Cryo-212][^cite_note-\:HKH2019Water-213] Around 2050, people living in the [Ganges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ganges) and [Indus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus) river basins (where up to 60% of non-monsoon irrigation comes from the glaciers)[^cite_note-Biemans2019-214] may be faced with severe water scarcity due to both climate and socioeconomic reasons.[^cite_note-AR6_WGII_Chapter10-210]<sup>: 1486 </sup>

By 2030, major Indian cities such as [Mumbai](19189.md), [Kolkata](47905.md), [Cuttack](1776398.md), and [Kochi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kochi) are expected to end up with much of their territory below the tide level.[^cite_note-215] In Mumbai alone, failing to adapt to this would result in damages of US\$112–162 billion by 2050, which would nearly triple by 2070. [Sea level rise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sea_level_rise) in Bangladesh will displace 0.9–2.1 million people by 2050 and may force the relocation of up to one third of power plants by 2030.[^cite_note-AR6_WGII_Chapter10-210] [Food security](216361.md) will become more uneven, and some South Asian countries could experience significant social impacts from global food price volatility.[^cite_note-AR6_WGII_Chapter10-210]<sup>: 1494 </sup> [Infectious diarrhoea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infectious_diarrhoea) mortality and [dengue fever](39669.md) incidence is also likely to increase across South Asia.[^cite_note-AR6_WGII_Chapter10-210]<sup>: 1508 </sup> Parts of South Asia would also reach "critical health thresholds" for [heat stress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heat_stress) under all but the lowest-emission [climate change scenarios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_scenario).[^cite_note-AR6_WGII_Chapter10-210]<sup>: 1465 </sup> Under the high-emission scenario, 40 million people (nearly 2% of the South Asian population) may be driven to internal migration by 2050 due to climate change.[^cite_note-AR6_WGII_Chapter10-210]<sup>: 1469 </sup>



Observed glacier mass loss in the [Hindu Kush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu_Kush) [Himalayas](37966.md) region since the 20th century

[^cite_note-\:HKH2019Cryo-212]

India is estimated to have the world's highest [social cost of carbon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_cost_of_carbon) - meaning that it experiences the greatest impact from [greenhouse gas emissions](9528025.md).[^cite_note-Guardian-216] Other estimates describe Bangladesh as the country most likely to be the worst-affected.[^cite_note-217][^cite_note-218][^cite_note-219] In the 2017 edition of [Germanwatch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germanwatch)'s *Climate Risk Index*, Bangladesh and Pakistan ranked sixth and seventh respectively as the countries most affected by climate change in the period from 1996 to 2015, while India ranked fourth among the list of countries most affected by climate change in 2015.[^cite_note-220] Some research suggests that South Asia as a whole would lose 2% of its [GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GDP) to climate change by 2050, while these losses would approach 9% by the end of the century under the most intense climate change scenario.[^cite_note-AR6_WGII_Chapter10-210]<sup>: 1468 </sup>

### Regions

Main category: [Regions of South Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category\:Regions_of_South_Asia)

#### Northern South Asia

[Ethnolinguistically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnolinguistic_group), [Northern South Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_South_Asia) is predominantly [Indo-Aryan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Aryan_peoples),[^cite_note-221][^cite_note-222] along with [Iranic populations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranic_peoples) in Afghanistan and [Balochistan](8431743.md), and diverse linguistic communities near the Himalayas.[^cite_note-223][^cite_note-224] Its borders have been heavily contested (primarily [between India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territorial_disputes_of_India) and its neighbours [Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Pakistani_border_dispute) and [China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sino-Indian_border_dispute), as well as [separatist movements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Insurgency_in_Northeast_India) in Northeast India.)[^cite_note-225][^cite_note-\:03-226][^cite_note-227] This tension in the region has contributed to difficulties in sharing river waters among Northern South Asian countries;[^cite_note-228] climate change is projected to exacerbate the issue.[^cite_note-229][^cite_note-230] Dominated by the [Indo-Gangetic Plain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Gangetic_Plain), the region is home to about half a billion people and is the poorest South Asian subregion.[^cite_note-\:03-226]

#### Northwestern South Asia

Northwestern South Asia is the site of many of the first civilisations of the world, such as the [Indus Valley Civilisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_Valley_Civilisation).[^cite_note-231][^cite_note-232] It was historically the most conquered region of South Asia because it is the first region that invading armies coming from the west had to cross to enter the Indian subcontinent;[^cite_note-233] because of these many invasions, Northwestern South Asia has significant influences from various sources outside of South Asia, mainly from the [Muslim world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_world).[^cite_note-234]

#### Eastern South Asia

[Eastern South Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_South_Asia) includes [Bangladesh](3454.md), [Bhutan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhutan), [eastern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_India) and [northeastern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_India) parts of India, and [Nepal](171166.md). Major Hindu and Buddhist kingdoms and empires of the region include [Kikata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kikata_kingdom), [Videha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_the_Videhas), [Vṛji](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vajjika_League), [Magadha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magadha_\(Mahajanapada\)), [Nanda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nanda_Empire), [Gangaridai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gangaridai), [Mauryan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maurya_Empire), [Anga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anga), [Kalinga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalinga_\(historical_region\)), [Kamarupa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamarupa), [Samatata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samatata), [Kanva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kanva_dynasty), [Gupta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gupta_Empire), [Pala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pala_Empire), [Gauda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gauda_kingdom), [Sena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sena_Empire), [Khadga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khadga_dynasty), [Candra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Candra_dynasty), and [Deva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deva_dynasty). Geographically, it lies between the [Eastern Himalayas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Himalaya) and the [Bay of Bengal](47892.md). Two of the world's largest rivers, the [Ganges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ganges) and the [Brahmaputra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brahmaputra), flow into the sea through the [Bengal region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengal_region). The region includes the world's largest delta, the [Ganges Delta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ganges_Delta), and has a climate ranging from [alpine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alpine_climate) and [subalpine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montane_ecosystems#Subalpine_zone) to [subtropical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subtropics) and [tropical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropics). Since Nepal, Bhutan, Northeast India and parts of East India are [landlocked](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landlocked_country), the coastlines of Bangladesh and East India (in [West Bengal](34040.md) and [Odisha](250724.md)) serve as the principal gateways to the region. With more than 441 million inhabitants, Eastern South Asia is home to 6% of the world's population and 25% of South Asia's population.

#### Central-West Coastal South Asia

This section is an excerpt from [Western India § History](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_India#History).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Western_India\&action=edit)]

In ancient history, Western India was divided into three great states according to [Hwen Thsang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xuanzang), namely [Sindh](28972.md) (which comprised the whole valley of the [Indus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_River) from the [Punjab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjab_region) to the sea, including the [Delta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_River_Delta) and the island of [Kutch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kutch)), [Gurjaradesa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gurjaradesa) (which comprised Western [Rajputana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajputana) and the [Indian Desert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thar_Desert)), and Balabhi (which comprised the [peninsula of Gujarat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kathiawar), with a small portion of the adjacent coast).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECunningham1871248-235] Before the [partition of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partition_of_India), the now-Pakistani territories of [Sindh](28972.md) and [Balochistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balochistan_\(Pakistan\)) were also included in this region. In art history, the term normally covers just Gujarat and Rajasthan, which tend to move together in terms of style.[^cite_note-236] Western India has the [second-largest gross domestic product](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Indian_states_and_union_territories_by_GDP) of any region in India.

#### Central South Asia

This section is an excerpt from [Central India § History](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_India#History).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Central_India\&action=edit)]

The [Holkars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holkars) of [Indore](365670.md), [Scindias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scindia) of [Gwalior](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gwalior), [Puars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pawar) of [Dewas Junior](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dewas_Junior), [Dewas Senior](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dewas_Senior) and [Dhar State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhar_State) were powerful families of the [Maratha Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maratha_Empire) which were based in Central India. The territories that now comprises [Madhya Pradesh](47945.md) and [Chhattisgarh](47734.md) were ruled by numerous [princes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princely_State) who entered into [subsidiary alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subsidiary_alliance) with the [British](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Raj).

After [independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purna_Swaraj), the states of [Madhya Bharat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madhya_Bharat), [Vindhya Pradesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vindhya_Pradesh), and [Bhopal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhopal) were merged into Madhya Pradesh in 1956. In 2000, the new state of [Chhattisgarh](47734.md) was carved out of [Madhya Pradesh](47945.md).

#### Southern South Asia

[Southern South Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_South_Asia) includes [South India](678583.md), the [Maldives](19117.md), and [Sri Lanka](26750.md),[^cite_note-237][^cite_note-\:05-238] and is predominantly [Dravidian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dravidian_peoples).[^cite_note-239][^cite_note-240] Southern South Asia was a hub of global trade in ancient times because of its position in the important Indian Ocean corridor.[^cite_note-241]

Historically, governments throughout Southern South Asia adopted Sanskrit for public political expression beginning around 300 CE and ending around 1300, resulting in greater integration into the broader [Indian cultural sphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_India).[^cite_note-242] This significantly influenced the languages of the region, making all of the major Dravidian languages except for Tamil highly [Sanskritised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanskritisation_\(linguistics\)).[^cite_note-243][^cite_note-244] Tamil influence in the region is quite significant, with prominent empires such as the [Chola dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chola_dynasty) taking [Tamil culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamil_culture) to Sri Lanka and beyond South Asia, and Sri Lanka having an ancient [Tamil minority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sri_Lankan_Tamils) and a Dravidian-influenced majority language of [Sinhala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinhala_language).[^cite_note-245][^cite_note-246][^cite_note-247]

## Society

See also: [South Asian ethnic groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Asian_ethnic_groups)

### Population

The population of South Asia is about 1.938 billion which makes it the [most populated region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_continents_by_population#Regional_and_continental_\(sub\)totals_in_2013) in the world.[^cite_note-248] It is socially very mixed, consisting of many language groups and religions, and social practices in one region that are vastly different from those in another.[^cite_note-249]

### Languages

Main article: [Languages of South Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_South_Asia)

See also: [Hindustani-speaking world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindustani-speaking_world)



Ethno-linguistic distribution map of South Asia



A clickable map of the official language or lingua franca spoken in each state/province of South Asia excluding the Maldives. Indo-Aryan languages are in green, Iranic languages in dark green, Dravidian languages in purple, Tibeto-Burman languages in red, and Turkic languages in orange.

There are numerous languages in South Asia. The spoken languages of the region are largely based on geography and shared across religious boundaries, but the written script is sharply divided by religious boundaries. In particular, Muslims of South Asia such as in Afghanistan and Pakistan use the [Arabic alphabet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_alphabet) and Persian [Nastaliq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nasta%CA%BFl%C4%ABq_script). Till 1952, Muslim-majority Bangladesh (then known as East Pakistan) also mandated only the Nastaliq script, but after that adopted regional scripts and particularly Bengali, after the [Language Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengali_language_movement) for the adoption of Bengali as the official language of the then East Pakistan. Non-Muslims of South Asia, and some Muslims in India, on the other hand, use scripts such as those derived from [Brahmi script](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brahmi_script) for Indo-European languages and non-Brahmi scripts for [Dravidian languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dravidian_languages) and others.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBhatia2008122–127-253][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPandharipande2008419–423-254]

The [Nagari script](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nagari_script) has been the *[primus inter pares](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primus_inter_pares)* of the traditional South Asian scripts.[^cite_note-george-255] The [Devanagari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devanagari) script is used for over 120 South Asian languages,[^cite_note-devasilusa-256] including [Hindi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindi),[^cite_note-257] [Marathi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marathi_language), [Nepali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepali_language), [Pali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pali), [Konkani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konkani_language), [Bodo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bodo_language), [Sindhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sindhi_language), and [Maithili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maithili_language) among other languages and dialects, making it one of the most used and [adopted writing systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_writing_systems_by_adoption) in the world.[^cite_note-258] The Devanagari script is also used for classical Sanskrit texts.[^cite_note-devasilusa-256]

The largest spoken language in this region is [Hindustani language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindustani_language), followed by Bengali, Telugu, Tamil, Marathi, Gujarati, Kannada, and Punjabi.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBhatia2008122–127-253][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPandharipande2008419–423-254] In the modern era, new syncretic languages developed in the region such as [Urdu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urdu) that are used by the Muslim community of [northern South Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_South_Asia) (particularly Pakistan and northern states of India).[^cite_note-259] The Punjabi language spans three religions: Islam, Hinduism, and Sikhism. The spoken language is similar, but it is written in three scripts. The [Sikh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikhs) use [Gurmukhi alphabet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gurmukhi_alphabet), [Muslim Punjabis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_Punjabi) in [Pakistan](23235.md) use the [Nastaliq script](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nastaliq_script), while [Hindu Punjabis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu_Punjabi) in India use the [Gurmukhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gurmukhi) or [Nāgarī script](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N%C4%81gar%C4%AB_script). The Gurmukhi and Nāgarī scripts are distinct but close in their structure, but the Persian Nastaliq script is very different.[^cite_note-Bright1996p395-260]

[Sino-Tibetan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sino-Tibetan) languages are spoken across northern belts of the region in the [Himalayan](37966.md) areas, often using the [Tibetan script](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tibetan_script).[^cite_note-BRILL-261] These languages are predominantly spoken in Bhutan and Nepal as well as parts of Burma and northern India in the state of [Sikkim](140372.md) and the [Ladakh](303611.md) region.[^cite_note-Walter_de_Gruyter-262] The national language of Bhutan is [Dzongkha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dzongkha), while [Lepcha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lepcha_language), [Limbu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limbu_language), [Gurung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gurung), [Magar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magar_language), [Rai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rai_languages), [Newari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newar_language), [Tamang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamang_language), [Tshangla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tshangla), [Thakali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thakali_language), and [Sikkimese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikkimese_language) are also spoken in Bhutan, Nepal and Sikkim, and [Ladakhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ladakhi_language) is spoken in Ladakh.[^cite_note-Walter_de_Gruyter-262] Both [Buddhism](3267529.md) and [Bon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bon) are often predominant in areas where these languages are present.[^cite_note-Walter_de_Gruyter-262][^cite_note-BRILL-261] Some areas in [Gilgit-Baltistan](18807336.md) also speak [Balti language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balti_language), however speakers write with the [Urdu script](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urdu_alphabet).[^cite_note-BRILL-261] The Tibetan script fell out of use in Pakistani [Baltistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltistan) hundreds of years ago upon the region's adoption of [Islam](6037917.md).[^cite_note-BRILL-261]

English, with British spelling, is commonly used in urban areas and is a major economic [lingua franca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lingua_franca) of South Asia (see also [South Asian English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Asian_English)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBhatiaBaumgardner2008391–394-263]

### Religions

Main article: [Religion in South Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_South_Asia)

Further information: [Religion in Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Bangladesh), [Religion in Bhutan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Bhutan), [Religion in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_India), [Religion in Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Nepal), [Religion in Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Pakistan), and [Religion in Sri Lanka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Sri_Lanka)



Religious Diversity in South Asia by National Subdivision

Religion in British India 1871–1872 Census (includes modern-day India, Bangladesh, most of Pakistan and coastal Myanmar)

[^cite_note-264]

1. [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism) (73.1%)

2. [Islam](6037917.md) (21.4%)

3. [Buddhism and Jainism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_and_Jainism) (1.49%)

4. [Sikhism](27964.md) (0.62%)

5. [Christianity](5211.md) (0.47%)

6. Others (2.68%)

7. Religion not known (0.22%)

In 2010, South Asia had the world's largest population of [Hindus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindus), about 510 million [Muslims](19541.md),[^cite_note-pewforum.org-37] over 27 million [Sikhs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikhs), 35 million [Christians](18337522.md) and over 25 million [Buddhists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhists).[^cite_note-Pew_Research_2012-35] Hindus make up about 68% or about 900 million and Muslims at 31% or 510 million of the overall South Asia population,[^cite_note-265] while Buddhists, Jains, Christians, and Sikhs constitute most of the rest. The Hindus, Buddhists, Jains, Sikhs, and Christians are concentrated in India, Nepal, Sri Lanka, and Bhutan, while the Muslims are concentrated in Afghanistan (99%), Bangladesh (90%), Pakistan (96%), and Maldives (100%).[^cite_note-pewforum.org-37] With all major religions practised in the subcontinent, South Asia is known for its religious diversity and one of the most religiously diverse regions on earth.

[Indian religions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_religions) are the religions that originated in the Indian subcontinent; namely [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism), [Jainism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jainism), [Buddhism](3267529.md), and [Sikhism](27964.md). The Indian religions are distinct yet share terminology, concepts, goals and ideas, and from South Asia spread into East Asia and Southeast Asia.[^cite_note-EB-266] Early Christianity and Islam were introduced into coastal regions of South Asia by merchants who settled among the local populations. Later [Sindh](28972.md), [Balochistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balochistan_\(Pakistan\)), and parts of the [Punjab region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjab_region) saw conquest by the [Arab caliphates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ummayad) along with an influx of Muslims from Persia and Central Asia, which resulted in spread of both Shia and Sunni Islam in parts of northwestern region of South Asia. Subsequently, under the influence of Muslim rulers of the Islamic sultanates and the Mughal Empire, Islam spread in South Asia.[^cite_note-267][^cite_note-268] About [one-third of the world's Muslims are from South Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_South_Asia).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERuffle2013193-36][^cite_note-pew2Apr2015-38][^cite_note-dip8Jan2016-34]

### Migration

See also: [Immigration to Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_to_Bangladesh), [Immigration to India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_to_India), and [Immigration to Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_to_Pakistan)

[Ancient migrations into South Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peopling_of_India) shaped the region's unique demographics, and later migrations often corresponded with conquest, as with the [British diaspora in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Britons_in_India).

Contemporary immigration to South Asian countries often takes a uniquely regional form; for example, most [migrants in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_to_India) are from other South Asian countries,[^cite_note-282] and 97% of [Bangladeshi migrants in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladeshis_in_India) live in the Bangladesh-bordering regions of India ([East India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_India) and [Northeast India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_India)).[^cite_note-283]

#### Diaspora

This section is an excerpt from [South Asian diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Asian_diaspora).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=South_Asian_diaspora\&action=edit)]

The [South Asian diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Asian_diaspora) is the group of people whose ancestral origins lie in South Asia, but who live outside the region.[^cite_note-284] With more than 44 million people within the cultural [diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diaspora), it is one of the largest in the world.[^cite_note-285] The term encompasses all whose ancestral and cultural origins lie in the modern nations of [India](14533.md), [Pakistan](23235.md), [Bangladesh](3454.md), [Nepal](171166.md), [Sri Lanka](26750.md), [Bhutan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhutan), the [Maldives](19117.md), and [Nepal](171166.md).[^cite_note-286] The diaspora is considered a distinct ethnic group by many nations’ [censuses](6889.md), with it having the largest number of immigrants in the world.[^cite_note-287]

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of South Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_South_Asia)

See also: [Category\:Culture of South Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category\:Culture_of_South_Asia)

### Sports

Main articles: [Sport in South Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_South_Asia) and [South Asian physical culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Asian_physical_culture)



An [Afghan soldier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_soldier) playing cricket. Afghan refugees who lived in Pakistan and India brought the sport back to Afghanistan, and it is now one of the most popular sports in the country.

[^cite_note-\:3-190]

[Field hockey](10886.md) has been the official national sport of many South Asian nations, additionally these countries collectively claim the majority of medals in [field hockey at the Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Field_hockey_at_the_Summer_Olympics). However, [cricket](25675557.md) is the [most popular sport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cricket_in_South_Asia) in South Asia,[^cite_note-288] with 90% of the sport's worldwide fans being in the Indian subcontinent.[^cite_note-289] There are also [traditional games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_games_of_South_Asia), such as [kabaddi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabaddi) and [kho-kho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kho-kho), which are played across the region and officially at the [South Asian Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Asian_Games) and [Asian Games](39205.md);[^cite_note-290][^cite_note-291][^cite_note-292] the leagues created for these traditional sports (such as [Pro Kabaddi League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pro_Kabaddi_League) and [Ultimate Kho Kho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ultimate_Kho_Kho)) are some of the most-watched sports competitions in the region.[^cite_note-293][^cite_note-294]

### Cinema

This section is an excerpt from [South Asian cinema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Asian_cinema).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=South_Asian_cinema\&action=edit)]

Cinema is prominent in South Asia, with the [Bollywood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bollywood) (representing the most-spoken language in the region of [Hindi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindi)) and [South Indian film industries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_South_India) being the most dominant. Pakistan's [Lollywood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lollywood) also is growing, while historically, [Bengali cinema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_West_Bengal) was highly acclaimed by international film circles.
