In 2010, South Asia had the world's largest population of [Hindus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindus),[^cite_note-Religion_in_South_Asia_\:0-295] about 510 million [Muslims](19541.md),[^cite_note-Religion_in_South_Asia_\:0-295] over 27 million Sikhs, 35 million Christians and over 25 million [Buddhists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhists).[^cite_note-296] Hindus make up about 60 percent or about 900 million and Muslims at 34 percent or 510 million of the overall South Asia population,[^cite_note-297] while Buddhists, [Jains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jainism), [Zoroastrians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zoroastrianism), Sikhs, and [Christians](5211.md) constitute most of the rest. The Hindus, Buddhists, Jains, Sikhs, Zoroastrians, and Christians are concentrated in India, Nepal, Sri Lanka and Bhutan, while the Muslims are concentrated in Afghanistan (99%), Bangladesh (91%), Pakistan (96%) and Maldives (100%).[^cite_note-Religion_in_South_Asia_\:0-295]

[Indian religions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_religions) (also known as Dharmic religions) are the religions that originated in the [Indian subcontinent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_subcontinent); namely [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism), [Jainism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jainism), [Buddhism](3267529.md) and [Sikhism](27964.md).[^cite_note-Religion_in_South_Asia_EB-298] The Indian religions are distinct yet share terminology, concepts, goals and ideas, and from South Asia spread into East Asia and Southeast Asia.[^cite_note-Religion_in_South_Asia_EB-298] Early Christianity and Islam were introduced into coastal regions of South Asia by merchants who settled among the local populations. Later [Sindh](28972.md), [Balochistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balochistan_\(Pakistan\)), and parts of the [Punjab region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjab_region) saw conquest by the [Arab caliphates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ummayad) along with an influx of Muslims from Persia and Central Asia, which resulted in spread of both Shia and Sunni Islam in parts of northwestern region of South Asia. Subsequently, under the influence of Muslim rulers of the Islamic sultanates and the Mughal Empire, Islam spread in South Asia.[^cite_note-299][^cite_note-300] About [one-third of the world's Muslims are from South Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_South_Asia).[^cite_note-301][^cite_note-302][^cite_note-303]

## Economy

Main article: [Economy of South Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_South_Asia)

Further information: [Economy of Afghanistan](9895.md), [Economy of Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Bangladesh), [Economy of Bhutan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Bhutan), [Economy of India](1472206.md), [Economy of the Maldives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_the_Maldives), [Economy of Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Nepal), [Economy of Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Pakistan), and [Economy of Sri Lanka](27415.md)



Mumbai is the financial capital of India with GDP of \$400 billion

[^cite_note-304]



GDP per capita development in South Asia from 1950 to 2022

India is the largest economy in the region (US\$4.18 trillion) and makes up almost 82% of the South Asian economy; it is the [world's 6th largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(nominal\)) in nominal terms and the [world's 3rd largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(PPP\)) by purchasing power adjusted exchange rates (US\$17.64 trillion).[^cite_note-IMF-305] India is the member of [G-20 major economies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G-20_major_economies) and [BRICS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BRICS) from the region. It is the fastest-growing major economy in the world and one of the world's fastest registering a growth of 7.2% in FY 2022–23.[^cite_note-306]

India is followed by Bangladesh, which has a GDP of (\$446 billion). It has the fastest GDP growth rate in Asia. It is one of the emerging and growth-leading economies of the world, and is also listed among the [Next Eleven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_O'Neill,_Baron_O'Neill_of_Gatley#Next_Eleven) countries. It is also one of the fastest-growing middle-income countries. It has the [world's 33rd largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(nominal\)) GDP in nominal terms and is the [25th largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(PPP\)) by purchasing power adjusted exchange rates (\$1.476 trillion). Bangladesh's economic growth was 6.4% in 2022.[^cite_note-307] The next one is Pakistan, which has an economy of (\$340 billion). Next is [Sri Lanka](26750.md), which has the 2nd highest GDP per capita and the 4th largest economy in the region. According to a [World Bank](36752.md) report in 2015, driven by a strong expansion in India, coupled with favourable oil prices, from the last quarter of 2014 South Asia became the fastest-growing region in the world.[^cite_note-308]

While [in East Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_East_Asia), regional trade accounts for 50% of total trade, it accounts for only a little more than 5% in South Asia.[^cite_note-309] Certain parts of South Asia are significantly wealthier than others; the four Indian states of [Maharashtra](20629.md), [Tamil Nadu](29918.md), [Gujarat](53707.md), and [Karnataka](16880.md) are projected to account for almost 50% of India's GDP by 2030, while the five [South Indian states](678583.md) comprising 20% of India's population are expected to contribute 35% of India's GDP by 2030.[^cite_note-310]

### Poverty

According to the World Bank's 2011 report, based on 2005 ICP PPP, about 24.6% of the South Asian population was below the international poverty line of \$1.25/day.[^cite_note-316] Bhutan, Maldives and Sri Lanka had the lowest number of people below the poverty line, with 2.4%, 1.5% and 4.1% respectively.

According to the 2023 MPI (multidimensional poverty index) report by the UN, around 20% of South Asians are poor.[^cite_note-317]

51.7% of Afghanistan's population was under the MPI poverty threshold in 2019,[^cite_note-318] while 24.1% of Bangladesh's population was under the threshold in 2021.[^cite_note-319] India lifted 415 million people from MPI-poverty from 2005/06 to 2019/21; 16.4% of India's population was MPI-poor in 2019/2021, compared to 55.1% in 2005/2006.[^cite_note-\:2-183] 10% of India's population was under the international poverty line of \$2.15/day in 2021.[^cite_note-320]

### Stock exchanges

The major stock exchanges in the region are [National Stock Exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Stock_Exchange_of_India) (NSE) of India with market capitalisation of \$5.70 trillion ([5th largest in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_stock_exchanges)), [Bombay Stock Exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bombay_Stock_Exchange) (BSE) of India with market capitalisation of \$5.30 trillion (7th largest in the world), [Dhaka Stock Exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhaka_Stock_Exchange) (DSE) of Bangladesh, [Colombo Stock Exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colombo_Stock_Exchange) (CSE) of Sri Lanka, and [Pakistan Stock Exchange](49066780.md) (PSX) with market capitalisation of \$72 billion.[^cite_note-330] Economic data is sourced from the [International Monetary Fund](15251.md), current as of April 2017, and is given in US dollars.[^cite_note-auto-331]

## Education

See also: [History of education in the Indian subcontinent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_education_in_the_Indian_subcontinent)



[Durbar High School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Durbar_High_School), oldest secondary school of [Nepal](171166.md), established in 1854 CE



Lower class school in Sri Lanka



College of Natural Resources, Royal University of Bhutan

One of the key challenges in assessing the quality of education in South Asia is the vast range of contextual difference across the region, complicating any attempt to compare between countries.[^cite_note-SZ13-332] In 2018, 11.3 million children at the primary level and 20.6 million children at the lower secondary level were out-of-school in South Asia, while millions of children completed primary education without mastering the foundational skills of basic numeracy and literacy.[^cite_note-ESAFS-333]

According to UNESCO, 241 million children between six and fourteen years or 81 per cent of the total were not learning in Southern and Central Asia in 2017. Only sub-Saharan Africa had a higher rate of children not learning. Two-thirds of these children were in school, sitting in classrooms. Only 19% of children attending primary and lower secondary schools attain a minimum proficiency level in reading and mathematics.[^cite_note-MoreT-334][^cite_note-SZ3-335] According to a citizen-led assessment, only 48% in Indian public schools and 46% of children in Pakistan public schools could read a class two level text by the time they reached class five.[^cite_note-336][^cite_note-SZ3-335] This poor quality of education in turn has contributed to some of the highest drop-out rates in the world, while over half of the students complete secondary school with acquiring requisite skills.[^cite_note-SZ3-335]

In South Asia, classrooms are teacher-centred and rote-based, while children are often subjected to corporal punishment and discrimination.[^cite_note-ESAFS-333] Different South Asian countries have different education structures. While by 2018 India and Pakistan has two of the most developed and increasingly decentralised education systems, Bangladesh still had a highly centralised system, and Nepal is in a state of transition from a centralised to a decentralised system.[^cite_note-SZ13-332] In most South Asian countries children's education is theoretically free; the exceptions are the Maldives, where there is no constitutionally guaranteed free education, as well as Bhutan and Nepal, where fees are charged by primary schools. But parents are still faced with unmanageable secondary financial demands, including private tuition to make up for the inadequacies of the education system.[^cite_note-Oxfam4-337]

The larger and poorer countries in the region, like India and Bangladesh, struggle financially to get sufficient resources to sustain an education system required for their vast populations, with an added challenge of getting large numbers of out-of-school children enrolled into schools.[^cite_note-SZ13-332] Their capacity to deliver inclusive and equitable quality education is limited by low levels of public finance for education,[^cite_note-ESAFS-333] while the smaller emerging middle-income countries like Sri Lanka, Maldives, and Bhutan have been able to achieve universal primary school completion, and are in a better position to focus on quality of education.[^cite_note-SZ13-332]

Children's education in the region is also adversely affected by natural and human-made crises including natural hazards, political instability, rising extremism and civil strife that makes it difficult to deliver educational services.[^cite_note-ESAFS-333] Afghanistan and India are among the top ten countries with the highest number of reported disasters due to natural hazards and conflict. The precarious security situation in Afghanistan is a big barrier in rolling out education programmes on a national scale.[^cite_note-SZ13-332]

According to UNICEF, girls face incredible hurdles to pursue their education in the region,[^cite_note-ESAFS-333] while UNESCO estimated in 2005 that 24 million girls of primary-school age in the region were not receiving any formal education.[^cite_note-338][^cite_note-339] Between 1900 and 2005, most of the countries in the region had shown progress in girls' education with Sri Lanka and the Maldives significantly ahead of the others, while the gender gap in education has widened in Pakistan and Afghanistan. Bangladesh made the greatest progress in the region in the period increasing girls' secondary school enrollment from 13 per cent to 56 per cent in ten years.[^cite_note-ERC-340][^cite_note-Oxfam2-341]

With about 21 million students in 700 universities and 40 thousand colleges India had one of the largest higher education systems in the world in 2011, accounting for 86% of all higher-level students in South Asia. Bangladesh (two million) and Pakistan (1.8 million) stood at distant second and third positions in the region. In Nepal (390,000) and Sri Lanka (230,00) the numbers were much smaller. Bhutan with only one university and Maldives with none hardly had between them about 7000 students in higher education in 2011. The gross enrollment ratio in 2011 ranged from about 10% in Pakistan and Afghanistan to above 20% in India, much below the global average of 31%.[^cite_note-Tilak-342]

## Health and nutrition



Child getting vaccine in Bangladesh under the [Expanded Programme on Immunization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expanded_Programme_on_Immunization) (EPI)

According to [World Health Organization](33583.md) (WHO), South Asia is home to two out of the three countries in the world still affected by polio, Pakistan and Afghanistan, with 306 & 28 polio cases registered in 2014 respectively.[^cite_note-345] Attempts to eradicate polio have been badly hit by opposition from militants in both countries, who say the program is cover to spy on their operations. Their attacks on immunisation teams have claimed 78 lives since December 2012.[^cite_note-346]

The [World Bank](36752.md) estimates that India is one of the highest ranking countries in the world for the number of children suffering from [malnutrition](258979.md). The prevalence of underweight children in India is among the highest in the world and is nearly double that of [sub-Saharan Africa](27067.md) with dire consequences for mobility, mortality, productivity, and economic growth.[^cite_note-347]



A weekly child examination performed at a hospital in [Farah, Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farah,_Afghanistan)

According to the World Bank, 64% of South Asians lived in rural areas in 2022.[^cite_note-348] In 2008, about 75% of South Asia's poor lived in rural areas and most relied on agriculture for their livelihood[^cite_note-349] according to the UN's Food and Agricultural Organisation.

In 2021, approximately 330 million people in the region were malnourished.[^cite_note-350] A 2015 report says that Nepal reached both the WFS target as well as MDG and is moving towards bringing down the number of undernourished people to less than 5% of the population.[^cite_note-fao.org-322] Bangladesh reached the MDG target with the National Food Policy framework – with only 16.5% of the population undernourished. In India, the malnourished comprise just over 15 per cent of the population. While the number of malnourished people in the neighbourhood has shown a decline over the last 25 years, the number of under-nourished in Pakistan displays an upward trend. There were 28.7 million hungry in Pakistan in the 1990s – a number that has steadily increased to 41.3 million in 2015 with 22% of the population malnourished. Approximately 194.6 million people are undernourished in India, which accounts for the highest number of people suffering from hunger in any single country.[^cite_note-fao.org-322][^cite_note-351]

The 2006 report stated, "the low status of women in South Asian countries and their lack of nutritional knowledge are important determinants of high prevalence of underweight children in the region." Corruption and the lack of initiative on the part of the government has been one of the major problems associated with nutrition in India. Illiteracy in villages has been found to be one of the major issues that need more government attention. The report mentioned that although there has been a reduction in malnutrition due to the [Green Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Revolution) in South Asia, there is concern that South Asia has "inadequate feeding and caring practices for young children."[^cite_note-southasiahunger-352]

[Sri Lankan Parliament Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sri_Lankan_Parliament_Building), [Sri Jayawardenapura Kotte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sri_Jayawardenapura_Kotte), Sri Lanka

India is a [secular](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secular_state) [federative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federation) [parliamentary republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_system) with the [prime minister](24452.md) as head of government. With the most populous functional democracy in world[^cite_note-353] and the world's longest written constitution,[^cite_note-354][^cite_note-355] India has been stably sustaining the political system it adopted in 1950 with no regime change except that by a democratic election. India's sustained democratic freedoms are unique among the world's newer establishments. Since the [formation of its republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_Day_\(India\)) abolishing [British law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_India_Act_1935), it has remained a democracy with civil liberties, an active Supreme Court, and a largely independent press.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMetcalfMetcalf2006304-356] India leads region in [Democracy Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy_Index). It has a [multi-party system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multi-party_system) in its internal regional politics[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurnellCalvert1999125-357] whereas alternative transfer of powers to alliances of Indian [left-wing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Progressive_Alliance) and [right-wing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Democratic_Alliance) political parties in national government provide it with characteristics of a [two-party state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two-party_system).[^cite_note-Political_Parties_in_India-358] India has been facing notable internal religious conflicts and separatism however [consistently becoming more and more stable with time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_Fragile_States_Index).

The foundation of Pakistan lies in the [Pakistan movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_movement) which began in [colonial India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Raj) based on [Islamic nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_nationalism_in_South_Asia). Pakistan is a [federal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_republic) [parliamentary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_system) [Islamic republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_republic) and was the world's first country to adopt Islamic republic system to modify its republican status under its otherwise secular constitution in 1956. Pakistan's governance is one of the most conflicted in the world. The [military rule and the unstable governments in Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_coups_in_Pakistan) have become a concern for the South Asian region. Out of 22 appointed [Pakistani Prime ministers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Prime_Ministers_of_Pakistan), none of them have ever been able to complete a full term in office.[^cite_note-359] The nature of [Pakistani politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Pakistan) can be characterised as a [multi-party system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multi-party_system).

The [unitary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitary_state) [semi-presidential](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semi-presidential_system) constitutional republic of [Sri Lanka](26750.md) is the oldest sustained democracy in Asia. Tensions between the [Sinhalese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinhalese_people) and the [Tamils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sri_Lankan_Tamils) led to the emergence of the [Liberation Tigers of Tamil Eelam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberation_Tigers_of_Tamil_Eelam), a separatist [Sri Lankan Tamil militant group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sri_Lankan_Tamil_militant_groups) and the outbreak of the [Sri Lankan civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sri_Lankan_civil_war). The war, which ended in 2009, would undermine the country's stability for more than two and a half decades.[^cite_note-mod-defeat-360] Sri Lanka, however, has been leading the region in [HDI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Development_Index) with per capita GDP well ahead of India, Pakistan, and Bangladesh. Sri Lanka has a [multi-party system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multi-party_system), and the political situation in Sri Lanka has been dominated by an increasingly assertive ideology of [Sinhalese nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinhalese_nationalism).

[Bangladesh](3454.md) is a [unitary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitary_state) [parliamentary republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_system). The law of Bangladesh defines it as Islamic[^cite_note-361] as well as secular.[^cite_note-362] The nature of [Bangladeshi politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Bangladesh) can be characterised as a [multi-party system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multi-party_system). Bangladesh is a unitary state and [parliamentary democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_democracy).[^cite_note-363] Bangladesh also stands out as one of the few Muslim-majority democracies. "It is a moderate and generally secular and tolerant — though sometimes this is getting stretched at the moment — alternative to [violent extremism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Violent_extremism) in a very troubled part of the world", said [Dan Mozena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dan_Mozena), the US ambassador to Bangladesh. Although Bangladesh's legal code is secular, more citizens are embracing a conservative version of [Islam](6037917.md), with some pushing for [sharia law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sharia_law), analysts say. Experts say that the rise in conservatism reflects the influence of foreign-financed Islamic charities and the more austere version of Islam brought home by migrant workers in [Persian Gulf](24761.md) countries.[^cite_note-364]

By the 18th century, the Hindu [Gorkha Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gorkha_Kingdom) achieved the [unification of Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unification_of_Nepal). Hinduism became the state religion and Hindu laws were formulated as national policies. A small oligarchic group of Gorkha region based [Hindu Thakuri and Chhetri political families](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Nepali_political_clans) dominated the national politics, military and civic affairs until the abdication of the [Rana dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rana_dynasty) regime and [establishment of Parliamentary democratic system in 1951](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1951_Nepalese_revolution), which was twice suspended by Nepalese monarchs, in 1960 and 2005. It was the last Hindu state in world before [becoming a secular democratic republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_Day_\(Nepal\)) in 2008. The country's modern development suffered due to the various significant events like the [1990 Nepalese revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1990_Nepalese_revolution), 1996–2006 [Nepalese Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepalese_Civil_War), [April 2015 Nepal earthquake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/April_2015_Nepal_earthquake), and the [2015 Nepal blockade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Nepal_blockade) by India leading to the grave [2015–2017 Nepal humanitarian crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepal_humanitarian_crisis_\(2015-2017\)). There is also a huge turnover in the office of the [Prime Minister of Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_prime_ministers_of_Nepal) leading to serious concerns of [political instability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_instability). The country has been ranked one of the poor countries in terms of GDP per capita but it has one of the lowest levels of [hunger](149422.md) problem in South Asia.[^cite_note-Archived_copy-321] When the [stability of the country ensured as late as recent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_Fragile_States_Index), it has also made considerable progress in development indicators outpacing many other South Asian states.

[Afghanistan](737.md) has been a [unitary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitary_state) [theocratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theocracy) [Islamic](2646438.md) [emirate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emirate) since 2021. Afghanistan has been suffering from one of the most unstable regimes on earth as a result of [multiple foreign invasions, civil wars, revolutions and terrorist groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan). Persisting instability for decades have left the country's economy stagnated and torn and it remains one of the most poor and [least developed countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Least_developed_country) on the planet, leading to the influx of Afghan refugees to neighbouring countries like [Iran](14653.md).[^cite_note-AfgCIA-269]

[Bhutan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhutan) is a Buddhist state with a [constitutional monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_monarchy). The country has been ranked as the least corrupt and most peaceful country in the region, with the most economic freedom, in 2016.

[Maldives](19117.md) is a unitary presidential republic with [Sunni Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Islam) strictly as the state religion.

### Regional politics

See also: [War in Afghanistan (2001–2021)](19666611.md), [Indo-Pakistani wars and conflicts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Pakistani_wars_and_conflicts), and [South Asian Association for Regional Cooperation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Asian_Association_for_Regional_Cooperation)



Border disputes involving India against Pakistan and China have created complex geopolitical dynamics in South Asia.

[^cite_note-368]

India has been the [dominant geopolitical power in the region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regional_power)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBuzan200471-369][^cite_note-370][^cite_note-Regions_and_powers-371] and alone accounts for most part of the landmass, population, economy and military expenditure in the region.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAliAitchison2005-372] India is a [major economy](2206608.md), member of [G4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G4_nations), has world's [third highest military budget](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_military_expenditures)[^cite_note-373] and exerts strong [cultural](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_India) and [political influence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regional_hegemony) over the region.[^cite_note-college-374][^cite_note-ASAN-375] Sometimes referred as a [great power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_power) or [emerging superpower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emerging_superpower) primarily attributed to its large and expanding economic and military abilities, India acts as fulcrum of South Asia.[^cite_note-376][^cite_note-377]

[Bangladesh](3454.md), [Pakistan](23235.md), and [Sri Lanka](26750.md) are [middle powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_power) with sizeable populations and economies with significant impact on regional politics.[^cite_note-balancing-378][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBuzan200471,_99-379]



A 1959 map showing how most of Asia was communist (in red), with Pakistan (blue) being more aligned with capitalist powers than India.

[^cite_note-\:4-184]

&#x20;The resulting [Cold War dynamics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_War_in_Asia) impacted South Asian geopolitics in the late 20th century.

[^cite_note-380]

During the [Partition of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partition_of_India) in 1947, subsequent violence and territorial disputes left relations between India and Pakistan sour and very hostile[^cite_note-381] and [various confrontations and wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Pakistani_wars_and_conflicts) which largely shaped the politics of the region and contributed to the [emergence of Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh_Liberation_War) as an independent country.[^cite_note-382] With [Yugoslavia](34244.md), India founded the [Non-Aligned Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-Aligned_Movement) but later entered into an [agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Soviet_Treaty_of_Friendship_and_Cooperation) with the former [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) following Western support for Pakistan.[^cite_note-383] Amid the [Indo-Pakistani War of 1971](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Pakistani_War_of_1971), US sent its [USS *Enterprise*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Enterprise_\(CVN-65\)) to the Indian Ocean, which was perceived by India as a [nuclear threat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_threat).[^cite_note-Santosh-384] India's [nuclear test in 1974](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smiling_Buddha) pushed Pakistan's nuclear program,[^cite_note-385] which concluded in nuclear tests in [Chagai-I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chagai-I) in 1998, just 18 days after India's [series of nuclear tests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pokhran-II) for [thermonuclear weapons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thermonuclear_weapon).[^cite_note-Ministry_of_External_Affairs,_1998-386]

The [Soviet invasion of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet%E2%80%93Afghan_War) in 1979 accelerated efforts to form a union to restrengthen deteriorating regional security.[^cite_note-NIHCR_in_Islamabad-387] After agreements, the union, known as the [South Asian Association for Regional Cooperation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Asian_Association_for_Regional_Cooperation) (SAARC), was finally established in Dhaka in December 1985.[^cite_note-About_SAARC-388] However, deterioration of [India-Pakistan ties](4500730.md) have led India to emphasise more on sub-regional groups [SASEC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Asia_Subregional_Economic_Cooperation), [BIMSTEC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BIMSTEC), and [BBIN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBIN).

[Populism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Populism) is a general characteristic of internal politics of India.[^cite_note-389]

[^cite_note-Lowe2015-11]: &#x20;Lowe, John J. (2015). [*Participles in Rigvedic Sanskrit: The syntax and semantics of adjectival verb forms*](https://books.google.com/books?id=L07CBwAAQBAJ\&pg=PA2). [Oxford University Press](48518.md). pp. 1–2. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-0-19-100505-3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-0-19-100505-3). (The Rigveda) consists of 1,028 hymns (suktas), highly crafted poetic compositions originally intended for recital during rituals and for the invocation of and communication with the Indo-Aryan gods. Modern scholarly opinion largely agrees that these hymns were composed between around 1500 BCE and 1200 BCE, during the eastward migration of the Indo-Aryan tribes from the mountains of what is today northern Afghanistan across the Punjab into north India.

[^cite_note-Combined-4-Rigveda2-12]: &#x20; [Witzel 2003](#CITEREFWitzel2003), pp. 68–70: "It is known from internal evidence that the Vedic texts were orally composed in northern India, at first in the Greater Punjab and later on also in more eastern areas, including northern Bihar, between ca. 1500 BCE and ca. 500–400 BCE. The oldest text, the Rgveda, must have been more or less contemporary with the Mitanni texts of northern Syria/Iraq (1450–1350 BCE); \[...] The Vedic texts were orally composed and transmitted, without the use of script, in an unbroken line of transmission from teacher to student that was formalised early on. This ensured an impeccable textual transmission superior to the classical texts of other cultures; it is in fact something of a ''tape-recording'' of ca. 1500–500 BCE. Not just the actual words, but even the long-lost musical (tonal) accent (as in old Greek or in Japanese) has been preserved up to the present. \[...] The RV text was composed before the introduction and massive use of iron, that is before ca. 1200–1000 BCE." [Doniger 2014](#CITEREFDoniger2014), pp. xviii, 10: "A Chronology of Hinduism: ca. 1500–1000 BCE Rig Veda; ca. 1200–900 BCE Yajur Veda, Sama Veda and Atharva Veda \[...] Hindu texts began with the Rig Veda ('Knowledge of Verses'), composed in northwest India around 1500 BCE; the first of the three Vedas, it is the earliest extant text composed in Sanskrit, the language of ancient India." [Ludden 2014](#CITEREFLudden2014), p. [19](https://books.google.com/books?id=pBq9DwAAQBAJ\&pg=PA19): "In Punjab, a dry region with grasslands watered by five rivers (hence 'panch' and 'ab') draining the western Himalayas, one prehistoric culture left no material remains, but some of its ritual texts were preserved orally over the millennia. The culture is called Aryan, and evidence in its texts indicates that it spread slowly south-east, following the course of the Yamuna and Ganga Rivers. Its elite called itself Arya (pure) and distinguished themselves sharply from others. Aryans led kin groups organized as nomadic horse-herding tribes. Their ritual texts are called Vedas, composed in Sanskrit. [Vedic Sanskrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vedic_Sanskrit) is recorded only in hymns that were part of Vedic rituals to Aryan gods. To be Aryan apparently meant to belong to the elite among pastoral tribes. Texts that record Aryan culture are not precisely datable, but they seem to begin around 1200 BCE with four collections of Vedic hymns (Rg, Sama, Yajur, and Artharva)." [Dyson 2018](#CITEREFDyson2018), pp. [14–15](https://books.google.com/books?id=3TRtDwAAQBAJ\&pg=PA14): "Although the collapse of the Indus valley civilization is no longer believed to have been due to an 'Aryan invasion' it is widely thought that, at roughly the same time, or perhaps a few centuries later, new Indo-Aryan-speaking people and influences began to enter the subcontinent from the north-west. Detailed evidence is lacking. Nevertheless, a predecessor of the language that would eventually be called Sanskrit was probably introduced into the north-west sometime between 3,900 and 3,000 years ago. This language was related to one then spoken in eastern Iran; and both of these languages belonged to the Indo-European language family. \[...] It seems likely that various small-scale migrations were involved in the gradual introduction of the predecessor language and associated cultural characteristics. However, there may not have been a tight relationship between movements of people on the one hand, and changes in language and culture on the other. Moreover, the process whereby a dynamic new force gradually arose—a people with a distinct ideology who eventually seem to have referred to themselves as 'Arya'—was certainly two-way. That is, it involved a blending of new features which came from outside with other features—probably including some surviving Harappan influences—that were already present. Anyhow, it would be quite a few centuries before Sanskrit was written down. And the hymns and stories of the Arya people—especially the Vedas and the later Mahabharata and Ramayana epics—are poor guides as to historical events. Of course, the emerging Arya were to have a huge impact on the history of the subcontinent. Nevertheless, little is known about their early presence." [Robb 2011](#CITEREFRobb2011), pp. [46–](https://books.google.com/books?id=GQ-2VH1LO_EC\&pg=PA46): "The expansion of Aryan culture is supposed to have begun around 1500 BCE. It should not be thought that this Aryan emergence (though it implies some migration) necessarily meant either a sudden invasion of new peoples, or a complete break with earlier traditions. It comprises a set of cultural ideas and practices, upheld by a Sanskrit-speaking elite, or Aryans. The features of this society are recorded in the Vedas."

[^cite_note-fisher-partition-26]: &#x20;[Fisher 2018](#CITEREFFisher2018), pp. 173–174: "The partition of South Asia that produced India and West and East Pakistan resulted from years of bitter negotiations and recriminations ... The departing British also decreed that the hundreds of princes, who ruled one-third of the subcontinent and a quarter of its population, became legally independent, their status to be settled later. Geographical location, personal and popular sentiment, and substantial pressure and incentives from the new governments led almost all princes eventually to merge their domains into either Pakistan or India. \[...] Each new government asserted its exclusive sovereignty within its borders, realigning all territories, animals, plants, minerals, and all other natural and human-made resources as either Pakistani or Indian property, to be used for its national development... Simultaneously, the central civil and military services and judiciary split roughly along religious 'communal' lines, even as they divided movable government assets according to a negotiated formula: 22.7 percent for Pakistan and 77.3 percent for India."

[^cite_note-chatterji-partition-27]: &#x20;Chatterji, Joya; Washbrook, David (2013), "Introduction: Concepts and Questions", in Chatterji, Joya; Washbrook, David (eds.), *Routledge Handbook of the South Asian Diaspora*, London and New York: Routledge, [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-0-415-48010-9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-0-415-48010-9), [Joya Chatterji](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joya_Chatterji) describes how the partition of the British Indian empire into the new nation states of India and Pakistan produced new diaspora on a vast, and hitherto unprecedented, scale, but hints that the sheer magnitude of refugee movements in South Asia after 1947 must be understood in the context of pre-existing migratory flows within the partitioned regions (see also Chatterji 2013). She also demonstrates that the new national states of India and Pakistan were quickly drawn into trying to stem this migration. As they put into place laws designed to restrict the return of partition emigrants, this produced new dilemmas for both new nations in their treatment of 'overseas Indians'; and many of them lost their right to return to their places of origin in the subcontinent, and also their claims to full citizenship in host countries.

[^cite_note-talbot-sing-28]: &#x20;Talbot, Ian; Singh, Gurharpal (2009), [*The Partition of India*](https://books.google.com/books?id=utKmPQAACAAJ), Cambridge University Press, [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-0-521-85661-4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-0-521-85661-4), [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20161213073754/https://books.google.com/books?id=utKmPQAACAAJ) from the original on 13 December 2016, retrieved 15 November 2015, When the British divided and quit India in August 1947, they not only partitioned the subcontinent with the emergence of the two nations of India and Pakistan but also the provinces of Punjab and Bengal. ... Indeed for many the Indian subcontinent's division in August 1947 is seen as a unique event which defies comparative historical and conceptual analysis

[^cite_note-khan-great-partition-29]: &#x20;[Khan, Yasmin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yasmin_Khan) (2017) \[2007], *The Great Partition: The Making of India and Pakistan* (2nd ed.), New Haven and London: Yale University Press, p. 1, [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-0-300-23032-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-0-300-23032-1), South Asians learned that the British Indian empire would be partitioned on 3 June 1947. They heard about it on the radio, from relations and friends, by reading newspapers and, later, through government pamphlets. Among a population of almost four hundred million, where the vast majority live in the countryside, ploughing the land as landless peasants or sharecroppers, it is hardly surprising that many thousands, perhaps hundreds of thousands, did not hear the news for many weeks afterwards. For some, the butchery and forced relocation of the summer months of 1947 may have been the first that they knew about the creation of the two new states rising from the fragmentary and terminally weakened British empire in India

[^cite_note-EoMA-32]: &#x20;"[Indian Subcontinent](http://www.bookrags.com/research/indian-subcontinent-ema-03/) [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20120121100141/http://www.bookrags.com/research/indian-subcontinent-ema-03/) 21 January 2012 at the [Wayback Machine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wayback_Machine)" *Encyclopedia of Modern Asia* Macmillan Reference USA (Gale Group), 2006: "The area is divided between five major nation-states, Bangladesh, India, Nepal, Pakistan, and Sri Lanka, and includes as well the two small nations of Bhutan and the Maldives Republic...
